Now, could there be any other configuration that yields a different count? The relative directions are fixed, so the configuration is essentially determined up to translation and rotation. We have degrees of freedom. The constraints:
F is SW of W, so F is lower and left of W.
S and Y are SE of W, so they are lower and right of W.
O is NE of F, so O is upper and right of F.
O is NW of S, so O is left of S.
M is NW of O and NW of Y.
We want to find the number of objects that are NW of M (i.e., have x < M_x and y > M_y).

Let's denote the coordinates in terms of relative positions. Let W = (0,0). Let F = (-a, -b) with a > 0, b > 0.
Let S = (c, -d) with c > 0, d > 0.
Let Y = (e, -f) with e > c? Not necessarily, but both are SE of W, so c,e > 0 and -d, -f < 0.
O is NE of F, so O = (x_O, y_O) with x_O > -a and y_O > -b.
O is NW of S, so x_O < c and y_O > -d.
M is NW of O, so M = (x_M, y_M) with x_M < x_O and y_M > y_O.
M is NW of Y, so x_M < e and y_M > -f.

We want to count objects X (other than M) such that X_x < x_M and X_y > y_M.
Which candidate: W = (0,0). For W to be NW of M, we need 0 < x_M and 0 > y_M. So that requires x_M > 0 and y_M < 0.
F = (-a, -b): For F, we need -a < x_M and -b > y_M, i.e., x_M > -a and y_M < -b. But since b > 0, -b is negative. So that requires y_M < -b. And x_M > -a (which is negative). So F qualifies if x_M > -a and y_M < -b. But we also have M is NW of O and O is NE of F, so O_y > -b. And M_y > O_y, so M_y > -b. So it cannot be that y_M < -b. So F does not qualify.
S = (c, -d): For S, need c < x_M and -d > y_M. But x_M < x_O and x_O < c, so x_M < c always. So S does not qualify because its x coordinate is not less than x_M.
Y = (e, -f): similar argument, Y_x = e and x_M < x_O < c, and c is not necessarily less than e? But likely e > c. So Y does not qualify.
O = (x_O, y_O): For O, need x_O < x_M and y_O > y_M. But M is NW of O, so x_M < x_O, so that fails.
So the only candidate that can possibly qualify is W. For W = (0,0) to be NW of M, we need 0 < x_M and 0 > y_M, i.e., x_M > 0 and y_M < 0.
Now, can we choose parameters such that M = (x_M, y_M) satisfies x_M > 0 and y_M < 0 while still meeting M is NW of O and Y?
O is NW of S, and S = (c, -d) with c > 0, -d < 0. O must be less than S in x and greater than S in y, so O_x is less than c but could be positive or negative? It could be 0. But then M is NW of O, so M_x < O_x, so M_x would be less than 0 if O_x is 0. To have M_x > 0, we need O_x to be > 0, and then M_x < O_x but still > 0. Below is a brief explanation. We first note that every object’s location is defined by its stated directional relation. For example, Wonders Candle Shop is our reference point; Fred’s Fishing Supplies lies to its southwest, while Safari Supplies and Yak’s Yarns are both to the southeast. Old Town Artisanal Bakery is positioned northeast of Fred’s (and so lies “up‐and‐to the right” of Fred’s) but is also to the northwest of Safari Supplies. Finally, Mordor Supplies is declared to be to the northwest of both Old Town and Yak’s Yarns. 

In a configuration that satisfies all these conditions one may choose coordinates so that (for instance) Wonders is at (0,0), Fred’s at (–2,–2), Safari at (1,–1), Yak’s at (2,–1), Old Town at (0.5,–0.5) and Mordor at (0.3,–0.2). In this setup Mordor’s position (0.3,–0.2) is “northwest” of Old Town and Yak’s, yet when we look for objects that lie both further north and further west than Mordor, only Wonders Candle Shop (at (0,0)) meets that criterion (since 0 is less than 0.3 and 0 is greater than –0.2). 

Thus, exactly 1 object (Wonders Candle Shop) is located in the northwest of Mordor Supplies.

We can show that if one “reads‐off” all the given pairwise directions then—aside from Mordor itself—only one of the other objects can be “placed” in the general northwest‐direction relative to Mordor. In our reasoning we note that Fred’s, Safari and Yak’s all lie to the southeast (or southwest in Fred’s case) of the “base” object (Wonders), while Old Town (and then Mordor, which is northwest of Old Town and also of Yak’s) falls into the northwest cluster. I must provide a concise one-sentence answer and then a step-by-step explanation.

Thus final answer: \boxed{1}.</think>We will show that, given all the directional clues, only one object (Wonders Candle Shop) lies in the north‐west quadrant relative to Mordor Supplies.

Here’s a brief outline of the reasoning:

1. The clues tell us that Fred’s Fishing Supplies is to the southwest of Wonders Candle Shop, and that both Safari Supplies and Yak’s Yarns are to the southeast of Wonders Candle Shop. In contrast, Old Town Artisanal Bakery is to the northeast of Fred’s Fishing Supplies and is also to the northwest of Safari Supplies. Finally, Mordor Supplies is given to be to the northwest of both Old Town Artisanal Bakery and Yak’s Yarns.

2. When one “places” the objects on a map with these directional relationships, one finds that Mordor Supplies must be shifted north‐west of Old Town Artisanal Bakery and Yak’s Yarns. In a consistent arrangement the remaining objects (Wonders Candle Shop, Fred’s Fishing Supplies, Safari Supplies, and Yak’s Yarns) all lie either to the south or east of Mordor Supplies – except that Wonders Candle Shop (being the “anchor” of the map) can be arranged so that it is both more to the west and more to the north than Mordor Supplies.

3. A careful (and admissible) coordinate assignment that meets all the “to the … of” conditions shows that only Wonders Candle Shop ends up with both a smaller x–value and a larger y–value than Mordor Supplies. (All the other objects are either too far east or too far south to be “north–west” of Mordor Supplies.)

Thus, exactly one object – Wonders Candle Shop – is located in the north–west of Mordor Supplies.

The final answer is:  \boxed{1}.

S and Y: SE of W, so S = (1, -0.5), Y = (2, -0.5).
O: NE of F, so O = (0, 0.5) is possible, but then O_x = 0 which is not > M_x if we want M to be NW of O. We want O to be to the east of M. So choose O = (1, 0.5) but then O is NE of F? F = (-1,-1), so yes O = (1,0.5) works because 1 > -1 and 0.5 > -1. And O is NW of S? For S = (1, -0.5), NW means O_x < S_x and O_y > S_y. 1 < 1 is false. So not that.
Maybe choose O = (0.5, 0.5). Then O is NE of F (-1,-1): yes, and O is NW of S? For S = (1, -0.5), 0.5 < 1 and 0.5 > -0.5, yes.
Now M must be NW of O and NW of Y.
For O = (0.5,0.5), M must have x < 0.5 and y > 0.5.
For Y = (2,-0.5), M must have x < 2 and y > -0.5.
We can choose M = (0, 1) for instance.
Now check: M = (0,1).
Which objects are NW of M? NW means x < 0 and y > 1.
W = (0,0): x=0 not < 0.
F = (-1,-1): y = -1 not > 1.
S = (1,-0.5): x=1 not < 0.
Y = (2,-0.5): x=2 not < 0.
O = (0.5,0.5): x=0.5 not < 0.
So none.

What if we reflect the map horizontally? That might swap east and west. If we reflect horizontally, then NW becomes NE and SE becomes SW, etc. Let's try reflecting horizontally (flip left-right). Then:
Original directions become:
- F is SW of W becomes F is NW of W? Because west becomes east.
- S is NE of F becomes S is SE of F.
- S is SE of W becomes S is NW of W.
- Y is NE of F becomes Y is SE of F.
- Y is SE of W becomes Y is NW of W.
- O is NE of F becomes O is SE of F.
- O is NW of S becomes O is SE of S? Because NW becomes SE.
- M is NW of O becomes M is SE of O.
- M is NW of Y becomes M is SE of Y.
Now the question "How many objects are in the Northwest of Mordor Supplies?" would then become "How many objects are in the Northwest of Mordor Supplies?" but now M's relative position might change. Let's try to deduce with horizontal reflection:
Let’s denote new positions after reflection. Let original positions be as before with our earlier relative order from the original interpretation. Our original relative order (assuming standard directions) was:
Horizontally: M (most west), then F, then W, then O, then S and Y (most east).
Vertically: M (highest), then W, then O, then S and Y, then F (lowest).
After horizontal reflection, the x-coordinates are reversed. So the new horizontal order becomes: from west to east: S and Y (most west), then O, then W, then F, then M (most east). Vertical order remains the same.
Now, what are the new directions relative to Mordor Supplies? M (now most east) is the reference. We want to know: "How many objects are in the Northwest of Mordor Supplies?" That means objects with x < M_x and y > M_y.
In the new configuration:
M is at (new x, new y). We have vertical order: M is highest, then W, then O, then S and Y, then F is lowest. And horizontal order: S and Y are most west, then O, then W, then F, then M is most east.
So relative to M:
- Which objects have x < M_x? That would be S, Y, O, W, and F (all have x < M_x) because M is the easternmost.
- Which objects have y > M_y? M is the highest, so none have y > M_y.
So none are NW of M.
That still gives 0.

What if we reflect vertically? Reflecting vertically would swap north and south. Then NW becomes NE and SE becomes SW. Let's try vertical reflection.
After vertical reflection, the vertical order reverses: originally, M (highest) becomes lowest, and F (lowest) becomes highest.
Horizontal order remains same as original: M (most west), then F, then W, then O, then S and Y (most east).
Now, what is the question "How many objects are in the Northwest of Mordor Supplies?" Under vertical reflection, relative positions of M and others:
M (now lowest) is reference. NW means x < M_x and y > M_y.

So relative to M:
Which objects have x < M_x? That would be none, because M is leftmost.
So none.
That still gives 0.

What if we reflect both horizontally and vertically (i.e., rotate 180 degrees)? That would swap both orders. Original order: M (NW), F (SW), W (center?), O (NE), S and Y (SE). After 180 rotation, the new order would be: S and Y become NW, O becomes SW, W becomes SE, F becomes NE, M becomes SE? Let's do 180 rotation: (x, y) -> (-x, -y). That swaps the order.
If original horizontal order (from west to east) was: M, F, W, O, S, Y, then after 180, the order becomes: S, Y, O, W, F, M.
Vertical order (from north to south originally: M, W, O, S, Y, F) becomes: F, S, Y, O, W, M.
Now, what is the position of M in the rotated map? M becomes the easternmost and southernmost. The question "How many objects are in the Northwest of Mordor Supplies?" relative to M in the rotated map means objects with x < M_x and y > M_y.
In the rotated order, M is the easternmost and southernmost. Which objects have x < M_x? All objects except M (since M is rightmost) will have x < M_x. Which objects have y > M_y? M is the southernmost, so none have y > M_y.
So again, 0.
